<a id="q288"></a>

### Q288 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 39*

**Choose two answers.**

Though chronicling the heroism and sacrifice of the common soldier, Erich Remarque’s classic novel, All Quiet on the Western Front, is profoundly ________ and thus was banned by the Nazis since it implicitly opposed their vision of armed conquest.

- **A.** inspirational
- **B.** pacific
- **C.** prescient
- **D.** conciliatory
- **E.** prophetic
- **F.** clairvoyant

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **B. pacific**; **D. conciliatory**

**Source explanation**

The opening “Though” foreshadows a twist. The sentence means that although the novel describes “the heroism and sacrifice of the common soldier,” it is “profoundly _________,” where the blank is something that would be unexpected in a novel about brave soldiers. The sentence also says that the novel was banned because it “implicitly opposed,” or was against, “their vision of armed conquest.” Therefore, it must have an anti-armed conquest theme, something peaceful. “Pacific” and “conciliatory,” which both mean anti-war, are the correct answers. “Prescient,” “clairvoyant,” and “prophetic” all mean able to foresee the future, a trap related to the Nazis’s “vision of armed conquest.” The sentence isn’t talking about a supernatural vision, but rather about an overarching goal. It is true that “prophetic” can also mean “characteristic of prophets,” and many prophets in various religious traditions spoke out against what they saw as evil governments. However, the interpretation of “prophetic” as “resisting evil authority” is not what is found as even a secondary definition in standard dictionaries. More importantly, even if you accepted this definition, there is no synonymous or near-synonymous partner among the answer choices. Sentence Equivalence questions require two answers that produce equivalent sentences. Only “pacific” and “conciliatory” work.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 255.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [conciliatory](vocab.md#v-conciliatory)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q39. **Question:** PDF p. 192. **Answer key:** PDF p. 255.

</details>

---

<a id="q289"></a>

### Q289 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 40*

**Choose two answers.**

Just months from retirement, the disgraced executive was forced to make ________ exit from the company.

- **A.** a glorious
- **B.** a triumphant
- **C.** a boorish
- **D.** an ignominious
- **E.** a defiled
- **F.** an unseemly

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **D. an ignominious**; **F. an unseemly**

**Source explanation**

The blank should match “disgraced.” “Ignominious” and “unseemly” work well. Trap answer “defiled” does not appropriately describe an action (you could defile a holy place by doing something very inappropriate, but for a place or thing to be defiled, it should be very pure beforehand). “Glorious” and “triumphant” do not match the idea that the executive was “disgraced.” “Boorish,” which means ill-mannered, is incorrect because it doesn’t have a match.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF pp. 255–256.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [unseemly](vocab.md#v-unseemly)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q40. **Question:** PDF p. 192. **Answer key:** PDF p. 255.

</details>

<a id="q290"></a>

### Q290 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 42*

**Choose two answers.**

By framing the new law as a question of urgent safety rather than of privacy, the government obviated the need to pass through the standard channels of legislation, effectively ________ all formal dissent and relegating any would-be naysayer from a position of engaged activist to that of powerless bystander.

- **A.** curtailing
- **B.** undermining
- **C.** targeting
- **D.** lobbying
- **E.** instigating
- **F.** facilitating

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. curtailing**; **B. undermining**

**Source explanation**

The government “obviated” or avoided, the traditional “channels of legislation,” and by doing so is making “formal dissent” impossible. In other words, the government is “curtailing” or “undermining” such disagreement—words that both mean to prevent or undercut. “Targeting” is tempting, but the government isn’t aiming at or attacking formal dissent; rather, it is preventing or avoiding it entirely. “Lobbying,” “instigating,” and “facilitating,” although all words associated with political actions, do not have the correct meaning.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 256.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [curtailing](vocab.md#v-curtail) → `curtail`, [undermining](vocab.md#v-undermine) → `undermine`

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q42. **Question:** PDF pp. 192–193. **Answer key:** PDF p. 256.

</details>

<a id="q291"></a>

### Q291 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 45*

**Choose two answers.**

Always on the lookout for a shady deal or quick con, she became known and scorned as an ________ opportunist.

- **A.** unqualified
- **B.** unprincipled
- **C.** alluring
- **D.** unprecedented
- **E.** attractive
- **F.** unscrupulous

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **B. unprincipled**; **F. unscrupulous**

**Source explanation**

The woman is looking for “a shady deal or quick con,” making her someone who is looking to make money by any means necessary. While “alluring” and “attractive” are a pair of words with similar meanings, there is nothing in the sentence that suggests she is pleasing to the eye. Of the remaining words, “unprecedented” does not work, since there is no clue that she is the first opportunist, and “unqualified” fails as well, since there is no clue that she is not qualified as an opportunist. This leaves the correct pair of “unprincipled” and “unscrupulous,” both of which mean not acting with moral principles.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 257.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [unscrupulous](vocab.md#v-unscrupulous)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q45. **Question:** PDF p. 194. **Answer key:** PDF p. 257.

</details>

<a id="q292"></a>

### Q292 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 48*

**Choose two answers.**

Given the breadth and speed of social media, the only way celebrities can hope to conceal their foibles is by employing practices as ________ as those of a spy ring.

- **A.** draconian
- **B.** arduous
- **C.** conspicuous
- **D.** duplicitous
- **E.** fanciful
- **F.** cloaked

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **D. duplicitous**; **F. cloaked**

**Source explanation**

The sentence begins by suggesting that social media has fast and far-reaching effects, so celebrities wanting to “conceal their foibles” would need to do something that would resemble what a spy ring would do: be unobserved or deceitful. The correct answers are “duplicitous” and “cloaked,” both of which mean deceitful or concealed. While a spy ring might act in a way that is “draconian” (excessively harsh) or “arduous” (difficult or tiring), these words don’t fit with the sentence, nor do they have matching words among the answer choices. Both “conspicuous” (noticeable) and “fanciful” (overimaginative or unrealistic) describe practices that are the opposite of what a spy ring would employ.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 258.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [cloaked](vocab.md#v-cloak) → `cloak`, [duplicitous](vocab.md#v-duplicitous)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q48. **Question:** PDF p. 196. **Answer key:** PDF p. 258.

</details>

<a id="q293"></a>

### Q293 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 51*

**Choose two answers.**

Through ________ antics that flouted the conventions of the establishment, the Yippies of the late 1960s impressed themselves into the public consciousness; their behavior culminated in the instigation of riots in Chicago during the Democratic convention in 1968.

- **A.** fastidious
- **B.** socialist
- **C.** brazen
- **D.** anarchist
- **E.** communist
- **F.** insolent

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **C. brazen**; **F. insolent**

**Source explanation**

The sentence says that the “_________ antics” of the group “flouted” (openly ignored or disobeyed) “the conventions of the establishment.” “Brazen” and “insolent” can both mean to openly ignore or disobey and they are the correct answers. “Socialist” and “communist” form a tempting pair because people who adhere to either socialist or communist beliefs might well have disagreed with “the conventions of the establishment” in the United States in the 1960s, but neither term means to disobey. “Anarchist” is perhaps the most tempting wrong answer because anarchists do not believe in formal governments and are sometimes described as agitators or insurgents, but there is no match for “anarchist” among the choices, so it cannot be correct. “Fastidious,” or particularly concerned about detail or cleanliness, most certainly does not describe the behavior of the Yippies.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 259.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [brazen](vocab.md#v-brazen), [insolent](vocab.md#v-insolent)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q51. **Question:** PDF p. 198. **Answer key:** PDF p. 259.

</details>

<a id="q294"></a>

### Q294 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 53*

**Choose two answers.**

Many Enlightenment philosophers viewed Machiavelli’s book as a satire meant to expose and caricature the ________ claims to power of the very figures Machiavelli pretended to endorse.

- **A.** sarcastic
- **B.** specious
- **C.** spurious
- **D.** squalid
- **E.** stolid
- **F.** stoic

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **B. specious**; **C. spurious**

**Source explanation**

If Machiavelli only pretended to endorse “the _________ claims to power,” and if they were subject to “exposure and caricature,” they must have been not only illegitimate but ridiculous. None of the answers suggests ridiculousness, but two answers—“specious” and “spurious”—mean the claims are false. While not synonyms, both words describe claims that are superficially attractive, but in fact false. “Sarcastic” (mocking) might be a trap for someone who misses that the claims referred to by the blank are not made by Machiavelli but by those who originally made the claims to power. “Squalid” (dirty or dishonest) doesn’t fit the sentence or match any other answer choice. And while “stolid” and “stoic” both mean unemotional, the sentence offers no reason to believe that the claims are unemotional.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 260.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [specious](vocab.md#v-specious), [spurious](vocab.md#v-spurious)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q53. **Question:** PDF p. 198. **Answer key:** PDF p. 260.

</details>

<a id="q295"></a>

### Q295 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 54*

**Choose two answers.**

The defendant impressed the jurors as ________; they did not believe that a woman of her education and experience could possibly be as naïve as she acted.

- **A.** disingenuous
- **B.** guileless
- **C.** innocent
- **D.** accomplished
- **E.** artful
- **F.** culpable

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. disingenuous**; **E. artful**

**Source explanation**

This sentence is difficult because the word “impressed” is used to refer to making an impression in a negative way. The defendant did not make a positive impression; rather, the sentence suggests that she came across as not as naïve as she pretended to be. “Disingenuous” means precisely that and one meaning of “artful” is deceptive. The sentence does not suggest that the woman was “innocent,” and while she might have been “accomplished,” that’s not how the jurors thought of her actions. “Guileless” is a trap since it is a synonym of both ingenuous and artless, all three of which mean innocent and without deception; this meaning, however, is the opposite of what the blank requires. Finally, “culpable” means guilty and is an attractive trap, but goes further than the clues in the sentence; the woman was not naïve, but she was not necessarily guilty.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF pp. 260–261.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [disingenuous](vocab.md#v-disingenuous)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q54. **Question:** PDF pp. 198–199. **Answer key:** PDF p. 260.

</details>

<a id="q296"></a>

### Q296 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 55*

**Choose two answers.**

Crucial to fostering a realistic understanding of the potential boons—and perils—of the new drug will be a concerted effort to ________ the specific contexts and symptoms that render its use appropriate.

- **A.** furnish
- **B.** delineate
- **C.** outlaw
- **D.** transmute
- **E.** stipulate
- **F.** proscribe

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **B. delineate**; **E. stipulate**

**Source explanation**

A “realistic understanding of the potential boons— and perils,” or advantages and disadvantages, of the new drug requires that people know when they should and should not use it. In other words, the “contexts and symptoms” that are “appropriate” for taking the drug should be “delineated” or “stipulated,” both of which mean spelled out or specified. “Furnish,” which means to provide or to be a source of, might seem close, but one would not provide the times when the drug’s use is appropriate. Instead, the sentence suggests that people need to provide appropriate information about when the drugs should be used. “Outlaw” and “proscribe” form a pair, but one would be unable to ban or forbid “the contexts,” much less “the symptoms,” for using the drug. Finally, “transmute,” meaning to change in form, does not fit the context.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 261.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [delineate](vocab.md#v-delineate)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q55. **Question:** PDF p. 200. **Answer key:** PDF p. 261.

</details>

<a id="q297"></a>

### Q297 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 57*

**Choose two answers.**

Robert Gottlieb, who otherwise found much to admire in John Steinbeck, argued that Steinbeck was politically ________, offering an adolescent disaffection in place of settled judgment.

- **A.** naïve
- **B.** perspicacious
- **C.** contemptible
- **D.** keen
- **E.** callow
- **F.** disinterested

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. naïve**; **E. callow**

**Source explanation**

“Otherwise” suggests that Gottlieb did not find Steinbeck’s political views admirable. “Adolescent disaffection” suggests that Gottlieb found Steinbeck’s views in some sense immature, so look for negative words that mean something like unsophisticated. “Naïve” and “callow” are the best options. “Perspicacious” and “keen” both suggest insight, nearly opposite of what the sentence suggests. “Disinterested” is a tricky word here. It most commonly means unbiased, though it can also mean not interested (two very different meanings). Neither option makes a great sentence here, but just as important, none of the other answer choices has a similar meaning to either definition. “Contemptible” is negative, but with no suggestion of immaturity —make sure to use the clues provided without adding your own ideas.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF pp. 261–262.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [callow](vocab.md#v-callow)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q57. **Question:** PDF p. 200. **Answer key:** PDF p. 261.

</details>

<a id="q298"></a>

### Q298 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 58*

**Choose two answers.**

The mayor’s ________ speech turned the bipartisan issue—traffic reduction—into a three-month-long fight between former allies.

- **A.** alienating
- **B.** honest
- **C.** refreshing
- **D.** plodding
- **E.** divisive
- **F.** conventional

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. alienating**; **E. divisive**

**Source explanation**

“Bipartisan” in this context means an issue on which both sides agree. If the speech turns that issue into a “three-month-long fight,” then the word in the blank, which describes the speech, must be something pretty negative, pitting both sides against each other. “Alienating” and “divisive” are great matches, suggesting estrangement and hostility. A “refreshing” speech would have woken everyone up and a “plodding” speech would have put everyone to sleep, but neither would have turned friends against each other. “Conventional” and “honest” are both incorrect as well, since neither matches another answer choice.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 262.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [alienating](vocab.md#v-alienate) → `alienate`

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q58. **Question:** PDF pp. 200–201. **Answer key:** PDF p. 262.

</details>

<a id="q299"></a>

### Q299 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 61*

**Choose two answers.**

It is in the best interest of criminal defendants to appear ________ in front of the judge, showing that not all moral sympathy is lost on them.

- **A.** callous
- **B.** vindicated
- **C.** contrite
- **D.** penitential
- **E.** messianic
- **F.** pious

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **C. contrite**; **D. penitential**

**Source explanation**

Criminal defendants want to look good in front of the judge by “showing that not all moral sympathy is lost on them.” The defendants are not necessarily innocent, so “vindicated” doesn’t work. Neither would “pious,” meaning devoutly religious, or “messianic,” meaning fervent or passionate. And “callous” has the opposite tone of the intended meaning; a defendant appearing callous in front of a judge would be showing insensitivity for his or her actions. That leaves the correct answers, “contrite” and “penitential,” both of which mean remorseful or apologetic.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 263.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [contrite](vocab.md#v-contrite)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q61. **Question:** PDF p. 202. **Answer key:** PDF p. 263.

</details>

<a id="q300"></a>

### Q300 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 62*

**Choose two answers.**

After 25 years as an emergency room surgeon, his reaction during nearly any emergency could only be described as ________: he would evenly address the paramedics, evaluate the situation, and methodically work through his normal routine.

- **A.** qualified
- **B.** premeditated
- **C.** phlegmatic
- **D.** unflappable
- **E.** enraptured
- **F.** enthusiastic

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **C. phlegmatic**; **D. unflappable**

**Source explanation**

The surgeon worked “methodically” and addressed paramedics “evenly,” so his reactions in an emergency would be calm, cool, and in control. “Phlegmatic” means not excitable, calm, and composed, so it’s a good fit. “Unflappable” means not easily upset or perturbed, especially in a crisis. “Qualified” is a bit of a trap: he’s a qualified doctor, but to say that his reactions were “qualified” would imply that they were limited or restricted in some way. Someone methodical might be “premeditat[ing]” about certain things, but how can a reaction be “premeditated”? He couldn’t pre-plan his response to an emergency. “Enraptured” and “enthusiastic” would indicate emotional responses, but, in fact, the opposite was true: he showed little emotion and retained composure.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 263.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [phlegmatic](vocab.md#v-phlegmatic)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q62. **Question:** PDF pp. 202–203. **Answer key:** PDF p. 263.

</details>

<a id="q301"></a>

### Q301 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 64*

**Choose two answers.**

In response to a recent editorial slamming the agency’s newest advertising campaign, the agency spokesman denounced the piece as ________ adversarial motives, due to the editorialist’s position on the board of the agency’s primary competitor.

- **A.** stemming from
- **B.** producing
- **C.** typifying
- **D.** epitomized by
- **E.** engendered by
- **F.** creating

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. stemming from**; **E. engendered by**

**Source explanation**

If the editorialist is “on the board of the agency’s primary competitor,” then any attack on the ad campaign was probably written with ulterior motives. The blank should mean something like “coming from.” “Stemming from” and “engendered by” would both fit this meaning (“engendered” means created or produced). “Producing” and “creating” are a trap here; they would suggest that the editorial itself created the adversarial motives, which isn’t the case. The fact that the writer was on the board suggests that the adversarial motives led to the editorial, not vice versa. “Typifying” and “epitomized by” both suggest “being the ideal example of.” The sentence doesn’t suggest that the editorial is the perfect example of adversarial motives; it just implies that the piece was spawned by them.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 264.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [engendered](vocab.md#v-engender) → `engender`

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q64. **Question:** PDF p. 204. **Answer key:** PDF p. 264.

</details>

<a id="q302"></a>

### Q302 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 65*

**Choose two answers.**

In the week that followed the climber’s disappearance, Internet rumor mongers blogged a myriad of ________ reports of her demise, only to be embarrassed by the release of a dramatic video that showed her celebrating on the summit.

- **A.** apocryphal
- **B.** apocalyptic
- **C.** sentimental
- **D.** spurious
- **E.** saccharine
- **F.** scandalous

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. apocryphal**; **D. spurious**

**Source explanation**

The sentence states that “rumor mongers” were writing about the climber’s “demise” but were later embarrassed to discover she made it to the summit. This would suggest that the reports were untrue, which both “apocryphal” and “spurious” mean. The sentence does not support the idea that the reports were “sentimental,” “saccharine” (excessively sweet), or “scandalous.” Trap answer “apocalyptic” ignores the clues in the sentence and is inappropriate for an event involving only one person, even if the outcome was tragic.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 264.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [spurious](vocab.md#v-spurious)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q65. **Question:** PDF p. 204. **Answer key:** PDF p. 264.

</details>

<a id="q303"></a>

### Q303 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 67*

**Choose two answers.**

The tragedy—and the resultant horrific loss of life and damage to property —occurred because of his ________ approach to his duties, evinced by his slouching posture and cavalier attitude.

- **A.** murderous
- **B.** petty
- **C.** lax
- **D.** aristocratic
- **E.** barbarous
- **F.** slack

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **C. lax**; **F. slack**

**Source explanation**

The answers must parallel “slouching posture and cavalier attitude.” Note that cavalier is used here to mean offhand or disdainful. Thus, “aristocratic” is a bit of a trap answer, as are “murderous” and “barbarous,” which don’t match the clues—while this person’s actions caused horrific loss of life, it doesn’t sound as though that was his intention. “Petty” (of secondary or little importance) also doesn’t fit here. Thus, the correct answers are “lax” and “slack,” both of which mean not careful.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 265.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [lax](vocab.md#v-lax)

<a id="q304"></a>

### Q304 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 74*

**Choose two answers.**

To avoid a lengthy prison term, the convicted financier signed a binding document, delineating the millions in fines and restitution that she must pay, as well as another legal memorandum in which she ________ her role and financial interest in the hedge fund she had founded.

- **A.** abjured
- **B.** jeopardized
- **C.** reneged
- **D.** deposed
- **E.** censured
- **F.** forwent

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. abjured**; **F. forwent**

**Source explanation**

The founder did something bad enough to be threatened with a prison term. She is paying “fines and restitution” and she must also have to do something else negative (for her) relative to her role in the company. “Abjured” and “forwent” both work here; she is giving up her position and financial stake in the company. “Reneged” means to break a promise; she can’t “renege a role.” Similarly, a leader can be “deposed,” but you don’t depose a role. The hedge fund founder may be “censured” (probably worse), but it doesn’t make sense for her to “censure” (disapprove of, reprimand) her role. Finally, her actions may have “jeopardized” her role, but she wouldn’t sign a legal memorandum to do so.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 267.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [abjured](vocab.md#v-abjure) → `abjure`

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q74. **Question:** PDF pp. 208–209. **Answer key:** PDF p. 267.

</details>

<a id="q305"></a>

### Q305 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 75*

**Choose two answers.**

Some religious adherents follow the letter of their particular tradition while simultaneously ________ its most basic ethical tenets, a fact that may explain why so much violence is perpetrated in the name of love of and obedience to a faith.

- **A.** breaching
- **B.** obeying
- **C.** surpassing
- **D.** heeding
- **E.** contravening
- **F.** contracting

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. breaching**; **E. contravening**

**Source explanation**

The sentence sets up a contrast: “adherents follow the letter of their particular tradition while” doing something else. This contrast is reinforced in the second half of the sentence, which points to a contradiction between “violence” and “love.” The blank must mean something like going against “the most basic ethical tenets,” making “breaching” and “contravening” correct. “Obeying” and “heeding,” which both mean follow, are trap answers and are opposite the required meaning. Neither “surpassing” nor “contracting” fits the required meaning, nor do these choices have a pair among the other answers.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF pp. 267–268.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [contravening](vocab.md#v-contravene) → `contravene`

<a id="q306"></a>

### Q306 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 76*

**Choose two answers.**

It is perplexing that the number of PhD applicants in linguistics, so obviously a ________ field, has either grown or held steady in each of the past 15 years.

- **A.** moribund
- **B.** waxing
- **C.** burgeoning
- **D.** waning
- **E.** dissolute
- **F.** debased

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. moribund**; **D. waning**

**Source explanation**

The sentence indicates that the number of PhD applicants in linguistics “has either grown or held steady in each of the past 15 years” while also suggesting that this fact is surprising. This could be surprising for a number of reasons, but the only words that would pair in this sentence are “moribund,” which means dying, and “waning,” which means growing smaller. “Waxing” and “burgeoning” do form a pair, but if the field were growing, it would not be a surprise that the number of applicants has also grown. Neither “dissolute” nor “debased,” which both mean lacking in morals, fits the desired meaning of the blank.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 268.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [waning](vocab.md#v-wane) → `wane`

<a id="q307"></a>

### Q307 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 77*

**Choose two answers.**

Writers, particularly those of the contemplative persuasion, have always found the ________ nature of the mind—with its passing thoughts and inconstant moods—difficult to convey in language.

- **A.** inchoate
- **B.** essential
- **C.** vestigial
- **D.** ephemeral
- **E.** evasive
- **F.** fleeting

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **D. ephemeral**; **F. fleeting**

**Source explanation**

This sentence is about writers of the “contemplative persuasion,” meaning writers who are pensive and spend a lot of time in thought, and who have a tough time writing about a certain state of mind. The biggest clue to that state of mind is the phrase “passing thoughts and inconstant moods,” which means the answer will mean changeable, impermanent, or inconsistent. The best answers are “ephemeral” and “fleeting.” “Vestigial,” or a remnant of something, does not fit. “Essential” might work, but there’s no indication that the changeable aspects of the mind are necessary. “Evasive” (trying to avoid something) does not have the right meaning. “Inchoate,” meaning not fully formed, is tempting but does not form a pair with any other answer.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 268.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [ephemeral](vocab.md#v-ephemeral)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q77. **Question:** PDF p. 210. **Answer key:** PDF p. 268.

</details>

<a id="q308"></a>

### Q308 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 78*

**Choose two answers.**

Though the majority of rules in sports are enumerated in rulebooks, there is ________ code of conduct that relates to sportsmanship.

- **A.** a tacit
- **B.** an evanescent
- **C.** an incorrigible
- **D.** an unambiguous
- **E.** a blatant
- **F.** an implicit

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. a tacit**; **F. an implicit**

**Source explanation**

The word “Though” sets up a contrast, so the blank needs to be something that means the opposite of “enumerated in rulebooks.” Both “tacit” and “implicit” mean implied but not plainly expressed and are the correct answer. “Unambiguous” and “blatant” do form a pair, but have the opposite meaning of the one required for the blank. Finally, “evanescent,” which means short-lived, and “incorrigible,” which means not able to be corrected, do not form a pair, nor do they fit the meaning of the sentence.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 269.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [implicit](vocab.md#v-implicit), [tacit](vocab.md#v-tacit)

<a id="q309"></a>

### Q309 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 79*

**Choose two answers.**

Many people think that antibiotics are a cure-all, but these medications can actually ________ the problem; taken inconsistently, antibiotics can in fact strengthen bacterial strains.

- **A.** exacerbate
- **B.** ameliorate
- **C.** differentiate
- **D.** distort
- **E.** pathologize
- **F.** magnify

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. exacerbate**; **F. magnify**

**Source explanation**

While many people think antibiotics are great for everything (“a cure-all”), they can “actually” make the problem worse: they could “strengthen bacterial strains,” which is a bad thing. The two best answer choices are “exacerbate” and “magnify.” “Ameliorate,” or make better, is the exact opposite and so is incorrect. “Differentiate,” to distinguish, doesn’t fit the meaning either. “Distort” would mean that the problem is changed but not necessarily made worse. “Pathologize,” which means to view something as medically or psychologically unhealthy, is a tempting trap, as a bacterial infection is a an unhealthy condition, but the meaning doesn’t match the idea of making a problem worse.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 269.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [exacerbate](vocab.md#v-exacerbate)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q79. **Question:** PDF p. 212. **Answer key:** PDF p. 269.

</details>

<a id="q310"></a>

### Q310 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 80*

**Choose two answers.**

Sandra was entirely ________ by the crossword puzzle that, unlike the simple fill-in-the-blanks published on weekdays, was one of the more difficult cryptic crosswords only published on weekends.

- **A.** confounded
- **B.** flummoxed
- **C.** enraged
- **D.** smitten
- **E.** incensed
- **F.** impressed

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. confounded**; **B. flummoxed**

**Source explanation**

The puzzle in question is one of the “more difficult cryptic crosswords,” so one would expect Sandra to be, well, puzzled by it. “Enraged” and “incensed” are an intriguing pair, but there is no reason to believe Sandra was made angry by the puzzle. “Smitten” can’t work here because the correct idiom is smitten with. “Impressed” might work, but it doesn’t have a pair word (since “smitten” can’t be correct). “Confounded” and “flummoxed” both mean perplexed and are correct.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 269.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [confounded](vocab.md#v-confound) → `confound`, [flummoxed](vocab.md#v-flummoxed)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q80. **Question:** PDF p. 212. **Answer key:** PDF p. 269.

</details>

<a id="q311"></a>

### Q311 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 81*

**Choose two answers.**

While many teachers say they are keen on the idea of participatory pedagogy, they often have little understanding of what participatory practices entail, and are, in fact, ________ to change; even when they think they are doing otherwise, observations show that teachers perpetuate the teacher-centered classroom practices to which they have been habituated.

- **A.** amenable
- **B.** impervious
- **C.** inimical
- **D.** prone
- **E.** reconciled
- **F.** resigned

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **B. impervious**; **C. inimical**

**Source explanation**

The “while” at the beginning of this sentence indicates that the opening phrase, “teachers say they are keen on the idea of participatory pedagogy,” will be contrasted with the second idea, that teachers “often have little understanding of what participatory practices entail.” The part of the sentence following the semicolon confirms this reality with observations of how teachers actually act in classrooms. The blank, then, must be filled with a word describing how teachers are “in fact” likely to behave: not able or likely to change. Both “impervious” and “inimical” fit this meaning. “Amenable” and “prone” are not a true pair, nor do they match with the observations of how teachers actually behave. “Reconciled” and “resigned” are a pair that would mean the teachers are changing their behavior, but the sentence indicates that this is not so.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 269.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [inimical](vocab.md#v-inimical)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q81. **Question:** PDF p. 212. **Answer key:** PDF p. 269.

</details>

<a id="q312"></a>

### Q312 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 82*

**Choose two answers.**

The subject of the documentary was not bothered that the filmmaker received such ________ from the critics, but that none of the acclaim filtered down to him.

- **A.** opprobrium
- **B.** wealth
- **C.** fulmination
- **D.** approbation
- **E.** plaudits
- **F.** capital

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **D. approbation**; **E. plaudits**

**Source explanation**

The filmmaker received “acclaim,” while the subject of the documentary did not. “Approbation” and “plaudits” are both synonyms for acclaim and are correct. “Opprobrium” and “fulmination,” which both mean strong criticism or protest, reflect the opposite of the required meaning, while “wealth” and “capital” don’t fit the meaning of the sentence.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF pp. 269–270.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [approbation](vocab.md#v-approbation)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q82. **Question:** PDF pp. 212–213. **Answer key:** PDF p. 269.

</details>

<a id="q313"></a>

### Q313 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 83*

**Choose two answers.**

The Thin Blue Line, a documentary by Errol Morris, is one of a very few movies that has had a tangible effect on the real world; the film managed to ________ its subject, who had been on death row for a crime that Morris demonstrated that the man did not commit.

- **A.** exculpate
- **B.** incarcerate
- **C.** inter
- **D.** excuse
- **E.** manumit
- **F.** vindicate

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. exculpate**; **F. vindicate**

**Source explanation**

Morris’s film demonstrated that its subject “did not commit” the crime in question; in other words, the film demonstrated his innocence. Both “exculpate” and “vindicate” mean to clear someone of blame or suspicion and are a perfect match. “Incarcerate” is the opposite of what the sentence suggests, while “inter” means to place in a grave or tomb. “Excuse” means to lessen the blame or forgive someone for a fault, but not to prove they were not at fault in the first place. Finally, of the incorrect answer choices, “manumit” is closest in meaning, but is the act of freeing a slave, not a prisoner.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 270.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [exculpate](vocab.md#v-exculpate)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q83. **Question:** PDF p. 214. **Answer key:** PDF p. 270.

</details>

<a id="q314"></a>

### Q314 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 86*

**Choose two answers.**

In a way, the environmental movement can still be said to be ________ movement, for while it has been around for decades, only recently has it become a serious organization associated with political parties and platforms.

- **A.** an incipient
- **B.** a disorganized
- **C.** a nascent
- **D.** a nebulous
- **E.** an inconsequential
- **F.** an immaterial

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. an incipient**; **C. a nascent**

**Source explanation**

While the environmental movement has been around awhile, it has only recently become a “serious organization.” In other words, the movement can be said to be relatively new, in a way. The answer choices “disorganized” and “nebulous” don’t fit that meaning. The other pair, “inconsequential” and “immaterial,” is needlessly negative. “Incipient” and “nascent” capture the idea that, while the movement has been around for a while, it is only just now becoming a serious and relevant organization.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 271.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [nascent](vocab.md#v-nascent)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q86. **Question:** PDF pp. 214–215. **Answer key:** PDF p. 271.

</details>

<a id="q315"></a>

### Q315 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 87*

**Choose two answers.**

Einstein’s idea that electromagnetic radiation was divided into a finite number of “energy quanta” was purely experiential until it was theoretically ________ by the work of physicists such as Louis de Broglie and Werner Heisenberg.

- **A.** bolstered
- **B.** undermined
- **C.** condoned
- **D.** pardoned
- **E.** sabotaged
- **F.** buttressed

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. bolstered**; **F. buttressed**

**Source explanation**

It is critical to work out here whether the physicists mentioned at the end of the sentence were for or against Einstein’s theory. The sentence suggests that the idea went from “purely experiential” to “theoretically” something. There’s a contrast here, and the contrast is not between right and wrong. It is between something that has been shown by experiment and something that has been understood theoretically. The later scientists are providing support for Einstein. “Undermined” and “sabotaged” are negative, while “condoned” and “pardoned” don’t fit the meaning. Only “bolstered” and “buttressed” correctly express the idea of support.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 271.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [bolstered](vocab.md#v-bolster) → `bolster`, [buttressed](vocab.md#v-buttress) → `buttress`

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q87. **Question:** PDF p. 216. **Answer key:** PDF p. 271.

</details>

<a id="q316"></a>

### Q316 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 88*

**Choose two answers.**

The plan, according to law enforcement and judicial officials, was to keep the prisoner ________ during his court appearances, but the defense attorney argued that restraints would prejudice the jury.

- **A.** manacled
- **B.** malleable
- **C.** nettled
- **D.** fettered
- **E.** incensed
- **F.** incomparable

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. manacled**; **D. fettered**

**Source explanation**

They wanted to “keep the prisoner _________,” but the “restraints would prejudice the jury.” So the plan must have been to keep this prisoner restrained. “Manacled” and “fettered” are very close synonyms that mean chained or restrained and are, therefore, the correct pair. “Incensed,” meaning “angry,” pairs somewhat nicely with “nettled,” meaning irritated or annoyed. While they make a good pair, they do not mean restrained. Neither “malleable,” meaning bendable or easily influenced, nor “incomparable” fit the context of the sentence.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 272.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [manacled](vocab.md#v-manacle) → `manacle`

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q88. **Question:** PDF p. 216. **Answer key:** PDF p. 272.

</details>

<a id="q317"></a>

### Q317 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 89*

**Choose two answers.**

The painter was just as famous for his personality as for his work; unlike the many pretentious and egotistical men in his field, he was known to be entirely ________.

- **A.** artless
- **B.** shrewd
- **C.** ingenuous
- **D.** selfless
- **E.** adroit
- **F.** artful

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. artless**; **C. ingenuous**

**Source explanation**

The painter is “unlike” others who are “pretentious and egotistical.” In other words, he is not pretentious or egotistical. “Artless” and “ingenuous,” meaning without effort or pretentiousness, are perfect. “Shrewd,” “adroit,” and “artful” each mean clever or skillful and “selfless” means unselfish; none of these four traits contrasts with “pretentious and egotistical.”

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 272.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [artless](vocab.md#v-artless), [ingenuous](vocab.md#v-ingenuous)

<a id="q318"></a>

### Q318 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 90*

**Choose two answers.**

The newest romantic comedy wasn’t exactly bad, but simply ________; it had laughs, but they were all jokes most audience members had heard before.

- **A.** atrocious
- **B.** amusing
- **C.** trite
- **D.** hackneyed
- **E.** witty
- **F.** egregious

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **C. trite**; **D. hackneyed**

**Source explanation**

The movie “had laughs” but the jokes were old or unoriginal. The jokes, then, were not bad so much as “trite” or “hackneyed,” both of which mean unoriginal. “Atrocious” and “egregious” mean the same thing as bad, so they don’t work. And while “amusing” and “witty” make a pair, they are the opposite of what the sentence intends to say.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 272.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [hackneyed](vocab.md#v-hackneyed)

<a id="q319"></a>

### Q319 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 92*

**Choose two answers.**

Oftentimes, when administrators force teachers to cleave too closely to a federal curriculum, those teachers feel ________, because the mandatory curriculum curbs their sense of being creative and dynamic educators.

- **A.** crushed
- **B.** confounded
- **C.** thwarted
- **D.** undermined
- **E.** tormented
- **F.** walloped

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **C. thwarted**; **D. undermined**

**Source explanation**

The end of this sentence makes it clear that the teachers in question are not happy about having to “cleave,” or stick, to a prescribed curriculum because it prevented them from teaching in a “creative and dynamic” way. “Crushed,” “confounded,” “walloped,” and “tormented” are all negative, but all of them miss the meaning of the sentence (and no two of them make a good pair). On the other hand, “thwarted” and “undermined” fit the context: the teachers were prevented from teaching in the way they felt best.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 273.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [undermined](vocab.md#v-undermine) → `undermine`

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q92. **Question:** PDF p. 218. **Answer key:** PDF p. 273.

<a id="q320"></a>

### Q320 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 93*

**Choose two answers.**

The federal government knows that a certain level of financial stability can be attained by lowering interest rates, yet if it overuses this power, it risks losing its most reliable means of ________ a crisis.

- **A.** interring
- **B.** exacerbating
- **C.** annihilating
- **D.** palliating
- **E.** compounding
- **F.** assuaging

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **D. palliating**; **F. assuaging**

**Source explanation**

The government can lower interest rates to maintain “a certain level of financial stability,” but it can’t do so too often or it may not be able to handle or manage a future crisis. “Interring” is a difficult GRE word meaning to place a corpse in a grave or tomb. “Exacerbating” and “compounding” are the opposite of the desired meaning. Only “palliating” and “assuaging,” both of which mean easing or diminishing, correctly fit the meaning here; “annihilating” goes too far.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 273.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [assuaging](vocab.md#v-assuage) → `assuage`

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q93. **Question:** PDF p. 218. **Answer key:** PDF p. 273.

</details>

<a id="q321"></a>

### Q321 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 94*

**Choose two answers.**

Even though Mariposa loved taking on roles that involved a lot of lines, she was excited to be playing a more ________ character, requiring her to focus more on gesture and expression.

- **A.** laconic
- **B.** dramatic
- **C.** dejected
- **D.** curt
- **E.** mute
- **F.** melancholy

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. laconic**; **D. curt**

**Source explanation**

The words “[e]ven though” set up a contrast: Mariposa’s latest character does not have a lot of lines, so she will need to rely more on physical acting. Mariposa’s character might be quite “dramatic,” but that would not contrast with the roles that involved many lines. “Melancholy” and “dejected” imply sadness, but not necessarily a refusal to speak (Hamlet is pretty depressed and he talks all the time). “Mute” goes too far: one can’t be more mute; rather, one would be mute if that person had no lines at all. “Laconic” and “curt” both match the idea that her character is not very talkative. Note that “curt” has the sense of being rudely short with people, whereas “laconic” is not necessarily negative, and while they aren’t perfect synonyms, the two correct answer choices do provide a similar meaning to the sentence.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 273.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [laconic](vocab.md#v-laconic)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q94. **Question:** PDF pp. 218–219. **Answer key:** PDF p. 273.

</details>

<a id="q322"></a>

### Q322 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 95*

**Choose two answers.**

Because the United States has become a mature, established nation, the ________ nature of Thomas Paine’s political diatribes is now downplayed by government officials, who would vociferously denounce a contemporary version as seditious.

- **A.** pallid
- **B.** incendiary
- **C.** antithetical
- **D.** anemic
- **E.** demagogic
- **F.** deferential

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **B. incendiary**; **E. demagogic**

**Source explanation**

The sentence is quite complex. It can often be helpful to reduce such a sentence down to its main core—eliminating the tacked-on modifiers—and then add the extra information back in as needed. Doing so here leaves you with: “The _________ nature of Thomas Paine’s political diatribes is now downplayed by government officials.” The word “diatribes” denotes strongly negative speech or writing, and, as such, their “nature” would more likely be controversial or fiery than understated or restrained. “Pallid,” which means pale or weak, forms a close, but incorrect, pair with “anemic.” “Antithetical” works superficially—it means directly opposed or mutually incompatible but it doesn’t connote a nature that would need to be downplayed. “Deferential,” meaning respectful, is the opposite of the needed meaning. That leaves the correct pair: “incendiary,” which means inflammatory and provocative, and “demagogic,” which refers to verbiage intended to arouse strong negative sentiments (usually against an established power).

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF pp. 273–274.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [incendiary](vocab.md#v-incendiary)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q95. **Question:** PDF p. 220. **Answer key:** PDF p. 273.

</details>

---

<a id="q323"></a>

### Q323 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 96*

**Choose two answers.**

A professional spy, he always affected a ________ demeanor, but those who disliked him often characterized it as taciturn or brusque.

- **A.** phlegmatic
- **B.** histrionic
- **C.** hirsute
- **D.** melodramatic
- **E.** melancholic
- **F.** dispassionate

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. phlegmatic**; **F. dispassionate**

**Source explanation**

The people who disliked the spy thought his demeanor was “taciturn,” meaning reserved or uncommunicative, or “brusque,” meaning abrupt or blunt. Because those descriptives are given from the people who dislike the spy, look for words that describe those same qualities in a more neutral, or even positive, manner. “Histrionic” and “melodramatic” are a great pair, but, as is so often the case, they mean exactly the opposite of what the blank needs: overly theatrical and exaggerated. “Hirsute” is a tough word that actually means hairy, and there’s nothing to suggest that the spy is feeling “melancholic,” or sad. That leaves just two words. “Phlegmatic” means calm, cool, and collected, and “dispassionate” means unemotional and composed; both are more positive versions of “taciturn or brusque.”

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 275.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [phlegmatic](vocab.md#v-phlegmatic)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q96. **Question:** PDF p. 220. **Answer key:** PDF p. 275.

</details>

<a id="q324"></a>

### Q324 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 97*

**Choose two answers.**

Though Hamlet is famous for being ________, he still manages to go on something of a killing spree in Shakespeare’s play, proving that he is hardly paralyzed with depression.

- **A.** indecisive
- **B.** melancholy
- **C.** monological
- **D.** morose
- **E.** violent
- **F.** barbaric

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **B. melancholy**; **D. morose**

**Source explanation**

The key to this sentence comes at the very end, when the sentence says that Hamlet is not “paralyzed with depression,” or generally depressed. “Indecisive” and “monologic” both fit the famous character of Hamlet, but they don’t fit the sentence (nor do they make a pair). Similarly, “violent” and “barbaric” would be accurate in describing someone who committed a “killing spree,” but they don’t fit the blank. Only “melancholy” and “morose” match the idea that he was depressed.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 275.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [morose](vocab.md#v-morose)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q97. **Question:** PDF p. 220. **Answer key:** PDF p. 275.

<a id="q325"></a>

### Q325 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 98*

**Choose two answers.**

It’s worth wondering whether the increase in diagnoses of psychological disorders has caused us to see certain behaviors that were once considered normal as ________.

- **A.** importunate
- **B.** mythical
- **C.** unfortunate
- **D.** anomalous
- **E.** aberrant
- **F.** fabulous

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **D. anomalous**; **E. aberrant**

**Source explanation**

The word “once” signals a change in the direction of meaning. As the sentence ends with the idea that the behaviors in question “were once considered normal,” you need something for the blank that means uncommon. “Mythical” and “fabulous” may seem to fit, but they’re too extreme. “Anomalous” and “aberrant,” which both mean deviating from an expected standard, fit. “Importunate,” meaning persistent, may rhyme with “unfortunate,” but that’s about all they have in common.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 275.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [aberrant](vocab.md#v-aberrant), [anomalous](vocab.md#v-anomalous)

<a id="q326"></a>

### Q326 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 99*

**Choose two answers.**

Proust proved that the ________ can be the domain of the novel every bit as much as the fantastical can be.

- **A.** mundane
- **B.** literary
- **C.** bombastic
- **D.** cosmopolitan
- **E.** belletristic
- **F.** quotidian

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. mundane**; **F. quotidian**

**Source explanation**

While things that are “fantastical” can be “the domain of the novel,” Proust proved that other things can also be. The blank needs to contrast with “the fantastical.” “Mundane” and “quotidian” are the correct answer pair. “Cosmopolitan” means cultured or glamorous and is the opposite of what the sentence requires. “Bombastic” and “belletristic,” both of which mean pompous or ostentatious, do not contrast with “fantastical.” Finally, “literary” is a trap since a novel is a piece of literature.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 275.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [mundane](vocab.md#v-mundane)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q99. **Question:** PDF p. 222. **Answer key:** PDF p. 275.

<a id="q327"></a>

### Q327 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 101*

**Choose two answers.**

The reclusive boy was thought to be less than clever, but at sixteen he wrote a complex and beautiful symphony that at long last revealed him to be ________.

- **A.** dim
- **B.** musical
- **C.** monastic
- **D.** exceptional
- **E.** hermetic
- **F.** precocious

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **D. exceptional**; **F. precocious**

**Source explanation**

Though people used to believe the boy was “less than clever,” the “but” indicates that he actually was clever. In fact, he wrote a “complex and beautiful symphony” at the young age of 16, which suggests he is a prodigy, or child genius. “Precocious,” meaning advanced for his age, and “exceptional,” or extraordinary, are good descriptions for a child prodigy. “Musical” is a tempting answer, but the word doesn’t have anything to do with his cleverness, nor does it have a matching answer choice. “Monastic” and “hermetic” might appeal as answer choices, due to his “reclusive” nature, but they do not fit with other clues in the sentence. Note that “hermetic” doesn’t actually mean “like a hermit”; it means “airtight.”

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 276.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [precocious](vocab.md#v-precocious)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q101. **Question:** PDF p. 222. **Answer key:** PDF p. 276.

</details>

<a id="q328"></a>

### Q328 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 102*

**Choose two answers.**

Many poets ________ the primacy of meter over words: Stephen Fry, in his book, The Ode Less Traveled, argues that rhythm is essential in poetry, whereas deeper meaning is less important.

- **A.** stress
- **B.** acknowledge
- **C.** allow
- **D.** immolate
- **E.** underscore
- **F.** decry

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. stress**; **E. underscore**

**Source explanation**

The part of the sentence after the colon is an example illustrating the point made in the first part of the sentence. Since Fry “argues” that rhythm is more important than deeper meaning, the corresponding first part, “meter,” is more important than “words.” These poets are proclaiming “the primacy of meter over words.” The correct answers “stress” and “underscore” convey this meaning. The much milder pair, “acknowledge” and “allow,” doesn’t fit because their intensity doesn’t match that of “argues.” “Immolate” is a great GRE vocabulary word that means to burn as a sacrifice, which, although interesting, is definitely not the desired meaning here. “Decry” is another great word that means to publicly denounce, but it also does not fit.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 277.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [underscore](vocab.md#v-underscore)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q102. **Question:** PDF pp. 222–223. **Answer key:** PDF p. 277.

</details>

<a id="q329"></a>

### Q329 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 104*

**Choose two answers.**

Isherwood’s sympathy for communism during the interwar period was not only a reaction against fascism, but also a mark of his fellow feeling for the laboring classes and his ________ to engage as an equal with working people.

- **A.** disinclination
- **B.** hankering
- **C.** proclivity
- **D.** implacability
- **E.** unwillingness
- **F.** joviality

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **B. hankering**; **C. proclivity**

**Source explanation**

The word “and” signals agreement between two parts of the sentence. Isherwood had “fellow feeling for the laboring classes”; that is, he was sympathetic with working people and so had a desire to engage as an equal with them. “Hankering” and “proclivity” are the nearest synonyms. “Disinclination” and “unwillingness” are the opposite of the required meaning and “implacability,” which means not able to be appeased, also suggests a negative tone that is opposite what the sentence requires. “Joviality” (friendliness) is tempting because it is positive, but it does not fit the meaning of the blank.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 277.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [proclivity](vocab.md#v-proclivity)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q104. **Question:** PDF p. 224. **Answer key:** PDF p. 277.

</details>

<a id="q330"></a>

### Q330 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 105*

**Choose two answers.**

Academic freedom does not protect a professor’s classroom remarks on matters irrelevant to his subject, though it guarantees the professor considerable liberty of speech about matters ________ to his or her academic work.

- **A.** germane
- **B.** indifferent
- **C.** mimetic
- **D.** disinterested
- **E.** congruent
- **F.** pertinent

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. germane**; **F. pertinent**

**Source explanation**

“Though” implies that the matters about which a professor enjoys “liberty of speech” are opposite those “irrelevant to his subject,” so the blank might mean something like relevant. “Germane” and “pertinent” are the best synonyms. “Mimetic” and “congruent” are slightly related to the idea at hand—the former means copying (like a mime, for instance) and the latter means something like similar or in agreement. “Indifferent” and “disinterested” might seem like a pair, but “indifferent” means uninterested or apathetic, while “disinterested” means unbiased. Neither word fits nor do they form a pair.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 277.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [pertinent](vocab.md#v-pertinent)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q105. **Question:** PDF p. 224. **Answer key:** PDF p. 277.

</details>

<a id="q331"></a>

### Q331 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 107*

**Choose two answers.**

The tremendous wealth of ancient life on display as part of the Ancient Life of New York exhibit—billion-year-old blue-green bacteria from the Adirondacks, fossilized tree stumps and spiders from Schoharie County, trilobites from Oneida County, and armored fish from throughout the state —represents only a tiny fraction of the New York State Museum’s ________ collection of over one million specimens.

- **A.** piecemeal
- **B.** voluble
- **C.** exhaustive
- **D.** evergreen
- **E.** sweeping
- **F.** commanding

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **C. exhaustive**; **E. sweeping**

**Source explanation**

Since the tremendous wealth on display represents only a tiny fraction of the collection, and since the collection has “over one million specimens,” it is a very large collection indeed. You might anticipate an answer like huge. “Exhaustive,” which means including everything, and “sweeping,” which means extensive, both fit. If a collection is “piecemeal,” it would have been acquired piece by piece over time. Likely as that might be, there’s no clue in the sentence that would suggest this. “Voluble” means talkative and does not fit, nor does “evergreen,” which means fresh or popular. Finally, “commanding” means authoritative or imposing, and there’s no reason to believe that the collection commands authority over other collections.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 278.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [exhaustive](vocab.md#v-exhaustive)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q107. **Question:** PDF p. 226. **Answer key:** PDF p. 278.

</details>

<a id="q332"></a>

### Q332 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 108*

**Choose two answers.**

The judge’s keen eye for sussing out the pretension of the lawyers in her courtroom was surpassed only by the ________ wit with which she castigated them for it.

- **A.** sedulous
- **B.** mordant
- **C.** obtuse
- **D.** jurisprudent
- **E.** trenchant
- **F.** assiduous

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **B. mordant**; **E. trenchant**

**Source explanation**

The best clue in this sentence is its parallel structure. The judge’s keen eye “was surpassed only by the ______ wit with which she castigated them.” Keen means sharply discerning, so look for words that denote a sharpness, or bitingness, of wit. “Mordant” and “trenchant,” both of which are close in meaning to sharp and discerning, are best. Don’t be confused by the other set of synonyms, “assiduous” and “sedulous,” both of which have meanings close to diligent. “Jurisprudent,” which means skilled in the principles of law, is a trap for those thinking about words that might describe a judge. And someone who is “obtuse” would be slow-witted, a word that is the opposite of what the sentence intends.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 279.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [mordant](vocab.md#v-mordant), [trenchant](vocab.md#v-trenchant)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q108. **Question:** PDF p. 227. **Answer key:** PDF p. 279.

</details>

<a id="q333"></a>

### Q333 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 109*

**Choose two answers.**

The amount of self-abasement with which the inmate ________ the probation panel to be set free verged on the humiliating; nevertheless, the judges remained unmoved and he was ultimately sent back to his cell to serve another three years.

- **A.** beseeched
- **B.** chided
- **C.** snubbed
- **D.** conceded
- **E.** received
- **F.** supplicated

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. beseeched**; **F. supplicated**

**Source explanation**

The inmate is begging, pleading, imploring the probation panel to be set free. “Beseeched” and “supplicated” are closest to this meaning. A person might “receive” a group of individuals if he or she were welcoming them, but this doesn’t match the intended meaning of the sentence, nor does it match with any other of the answer choices. “Conceded” isn’t the right word either, nor would you concede a group of people (unless you were giving them away to someone else). Finally, both “chided” and “snubbed” have a negative connotation and are the opposite of how the inmate was acting towards the probation panel.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 279.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [supplicated](vocab.md#v-supplicate) → `supplicate`

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q109. **Question:** PDF p. 227. **Answer key:** PDF p. 279.

</details>

<a id="q334"></a>

### Q334 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 110*

**Choose two answers.**

The cult members treated their leader with ________ loyalty that verged on the obsessive and made them willing, should the need ever arise, to do so much as lay down their lives for him.

- **A.** a fanatical
- **B.** an arbitrary
- **C.** a fickle
- **D.** a mortifying
- **E.** a zealous
- **F.** an indeterminate

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. a fanatical**; **E. a zealous**

**Source explanation**

The loyalty of the cult members “verged on the obsessive”; use that same description to fill the blank. “Fanatical” and “zealous” are best here. An obsessive loyalty would definitely not be “arbitrary” or “fickle,” nor would it be “indeterminate,” or undefined. Finally, while others might be mortified by such loyalty, the loyalty itself would not be “mortifying.”

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 279.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [zealous](vocab.md#v-zealous)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q110. **Question:** PDF p. 227. **Answer key:** PDF p. 279.

</details>

<a id="q335"></a>

### Q335 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 113*

**Choose two answers.**

The new particles produced by CERN’s Large Hadron Collider are ________, lasting a millionth of a billionth of a billionth of a second before disintegrating into photons, quarks, or other particles.

- **A.** ephemeral
- **B.** infinitesimal
- **C.** myriad
- **D.** poignant
- **E.** fleeting
- **F.** countless

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **A. ephemeral**; **E. fleeting**

**Source explanation**

Something “lasting a millionth of a billionth of a billionth of a second” is very short-lived. “Ephemeral” and “fleeting” have just this meaning. In real life, the particles may be small (“infinitesimal”), there may be a lot of them (“myriad,” “countless”), and they may bring a tear to the eye of a few scientists (“poignant”), but none of these are indicated by clues in the sentence.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 280.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [ephemeral](vocab.md#v-ephemeral)

<a id="q336"></a>

### Q336 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 117*

**Choose two answers.**

The teacher was well-loved by students, but he never ________ the work of teaching; in fact, planning lessons and facilitating group process only exacerbated his deep-seated anxieties about preparation and public speaking.

- **A.** appreciated
- **B.** fancied
- **C.** abhorred
- **D.** relished
- **E.** detested
- **F.** ascertained

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **B. fancied**; **D. relished**

**Source explanation**

The teacher was “well-loved by students” but didn’t love or enjoy “the work of teaching.” In fact, he had “deep-seated anxieties” about certain aspects of teaching. “Fancied” and “relished” are the correct pair. Of the incorrect answer choices, “appreciated” is most similar to the correct answers, but there is no clue in the sentence that would suggest that the teacher did not appreciate the work he did. While “abhorred” and “detested” are synonyms, their meaning is the opposite of what the clues suggest. Finally, “ascertained” does not fit the required meaning, nor does it have a pair among the answers.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 281.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [relished](vocab.md#v-relish) → `relish`

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q117. **Question:** PDF p. 230. **Answer key:** PDF p. 281.

<a id="q337"></a>

### Q337 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 121*

**Choose two answers.**

Martin Luther King, Jr. was more ________ than is commonly thought today; it was only in the posthumous process of canonization that his more palatable, less far-reaching political and social visions became prominent.

- **A.** ineffective
- **B.** radical
- **C.** politic
- **D.** immoderate
- **E.** incongruous
- **F.** raucous

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **B. radical**; **D. immoderate**

**Source explanation**

There is a contrast between what Martin Luther King, Jr. was really like and how he is generally perceived today. The sentence contains two important clues. First, he went through a “process of canonization” posthumously (after his death), meaning that he was turned into a saint-like figure in the popular imagination. Second, during the process, his “more palatable” (pleasing or easy to agree with) “and less far-reaching political and social visions became prominent.” It must be true, then, that he had less palatable, more far-reaching visions that are now overlooked. The word in the blank should mean something like revolutionary or boundary-pushing. “Radical” and “immoderate” both fit this meaning. Ineffective,” “politic” (sensible), “incongruous” (not in harmony with the surroundings), and “raucous” (very loud or harsh) do not fit the required meaning.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 282.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [radical](vocab.md#v-radical)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q121. **Question:** PDF p. 232. **Answer key:** PDF p. 282.

</details>

<a id="q338"></a>

### Q338 · SE

*Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition — Sentence Equivalence, original question 124*

**Choose two answers.**

The so-called “reality” television show claimed to display the ________ side of the starlet’s life, but her daily routines were far too sensational for the claims to hold true.

- **A.** unusual
- **B.** predictable
- **C.** quotidian
- **D.** exotic
- **E.** mundane
- **F.** plastic

<details>
<summary>Answer, explanation, vocabulary &amp; source</summary>

**Correct answers:** **C. quotidian**; **E. mundane**

**Source explanation**

The show “claimed to display the _________ side of the starlet’s life,” but those claims cannot be true because “her daily endeavors were far too sensational.” The blank needs to say something along the lines of “unsensational.” The pair “unusual” and “exotic” means the opposite. “Plastic” has many meanings, none of which apply very well here, and it doesn’t have a pair. Finally, when down to “predictable,” “quotidian,” and “mundane,” choose the best pair. “Predictable” is a near miss here. It doesn’t necessarily mean “unsensational” because things could be predictably sensational. “Quotidian” means unsensational, everyday, just like “mundane,” so these are the best answer choices.

*Explanation: [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf), PDF p. 284.*

**Verified vocabulary:** [mundane](vocab.md#v-mundane)

- [Manhattan 5 lb., 3rd edition](gre_data/sources/manhattan.pdf) — Sentence Equivalence, Q124. **Question:** PDF p. 234. **Answer key:** PDF p. 284.

</details>
